## Document 004: 115年9月海關進出口貿易初步統計 (2026-10-08)

Report source: MOF
Date: 2026-10-08
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

115 年 9 月海關進出口貿易初步統計 
一、進出口貿易變動 
（一）出口 
9 月出口 872.2 億美元，創歷年單月新高，較上月增 5.9%，較上年
同月增 60.9%(按新臺幣計算增 68.2%)；第3 季出口 2,449.2 億美元，
較上年同季增 44.6%；累計 1 至 9 月 6,615.5 億美元，較上年同期增
46.2%(按新臺幣計算增 49.1%)。 
（二）進口 
9 月進口 635.9 億美元，亦創歷年單月新高，較上月增 5.8%，較上
年同月增 51.7%(按新臺幣計算增 58.6%)；第 3 季進口 1,818.0 億美
元，較上年同季增 44.3%；累計 1 至 9 月 5,010.0 億美元，較上年同
期增 41.3%(按新臺幣計算增 43.8%)。 
（三）出入超 
9 月出超 236.3 億美元，為單月新高，較上年同月增 113.4 億美元；
累計 1 至 9 月出超 1,605.5 億美元，較上年同期增 625.4 億美元。

### Page 2

二、出口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
由於創新科技應用商機持續擴展，推升相關供應鏈出貨動能，加上
消費性電子新品備貨需求與產品價格上漲效應之挹注， 9 月出口較
上年同月增 330.0 億美元(+60.9%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 資通與視聽產品出口 391.3 億美元(歷年單月次高)， 較上年同月增
198.6 億美元(+1.0 倍)， 其中電腦及其附屬單元增157.4 億美元(+1.1
倍)、儲存媒體增 17.6 億美元(+2.2 倍)、交換器及路由器增 10.5
億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
2. 電子零組件出口 311.3 億美元(歷年單月次高)， 較上年同月增97.1
億美元(+45.3%)，其中積體電路增 93.9 億美元(+46.1%)、印刷電
路增 1.4 億美元(+33.7%)、 電容器及電阻器增0.8 億美元(+32.0%)。 
3. 礦產品出口 22.6 億美元，較上年同月增 10.5 億美元(+86.4%)，其
中石油煉製品增 10.1 億美元(+99.0%)。 
4. 機械出口 27.4 億美元(歷年單月次高)，較上年同月增 6.4 億美元
(+30.7%)，其中生產半導體等機械增 2.7 億美元(+61.0%)、未列名
具特殊功能機械增 0.6 億美元(+73.6%)。 
5. 基本金屬及其製品出口 26.9 億美元，較上年同月增 5.0 億美元
(+22.9%)，其中銅及其製品增 2.5 億美元(+65.1%)、鋼鐵及其製品
增 1.4 億美元(+11.7%)。

（二）與上年同期比較 
1. 第 3 季出口較上年同季增 755.9 億美元(+44.6%)，以資通與視聽產
品增 367.6 億美元(+55.0%)、電子零組件增 308.4 億美元(+51.2%)
增加較多。 
2. 累計 1 至 9 月出口較上年同期增 2,089.8 億美元(+46.2%)，其中資
通與視聽產品增 1,222.2 億美元(+72.4%)、電子零組件增 726.6 億
美元(+45.7%)。

等出現結構性轉變，爰另予併計。

三、進口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
隨 AI 產業鏈全球分工與出口引申需求擴增，電子零組件、資通產品
及半導體設備進口維持強勁，加上國際農工原料行情處於高檔，9
月進口較上年同月增 216.7 億美元(+51.7%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 農工原料進口440.5 億美元， 較上年同月增163.9億美元(+59.3%)，
其中電子零組件增 112.6 億美元(+82.5%)、電腦之零附件增 19.4
億美元(+1.5 倍)、礦產品增 17.1 億美元(+38.9%)。 
2. 資本設備進口 128.0 億美元， 較上年同月增 36.0 億美元(+39.1%)，
其中資通設備增 18.4 億美元(+75.0%)、半導體設備增 12.4 億美元
(+34.9%)，交通運輸設備減 1.4 億美元(-46.4%)。 
3. 消費品進口 62.1 億美元，較上年同月增 16.5 億美元(+36.0%)，其
中固態非揮發性儲存裝置增 16.4 億美元(+5.3 倍)、筆電增 0.5 億
美元(+43.8%)，藥粧用品減 1.2 億美元(-16.6%)。

四、主要出口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
9 月對東協出口創新高，對美國、陸港為單月次高 
1. 對美國出口 274.6 億美元，較上年同月增 141.5 億美元(+1.1 倍)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 124.1 億美元(+1.3 倍)、 電子零組件增10.1
億美元(+1.3 倍)、機械增 2.2 億美元(+42.3%)。 
2. 對中國大陸與香港出口 206.2 億美元，較上年同月增 47.8 億美元
(+30.2%)，其中電子零組件增 34.9 億美元(+30.5%)、資通與視聽
產品增 5.7 億美元(+39.0%)、機械增 1.9 億美元(+43.7%)。 
3. 對東協出口 188.8 億美元，較上年同月增 91.2 億美元(+93.4%)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 49.7 億美元(+2.2 倍)、電子零組件增 32.0
億美元(+63.3%)、礦產品增 4.8 億美元(+1.4 倍)。 
4. 對歐洲出口 45.3 億美元，較上年同月增 7.7 億美元(+20.6%)，其
中資通與視聽產品增 5.7 億美元(+34.1%)、電子零組件增 2.2 億美
元(+56.6%)，基本金屬及其製品減 0.8 億美元(-20.5%)。 
5. 對日本出口 30.7 億美元，較上年同月增 3.6 億美元(+13.4%)，其
中電子零組件增 1.5 億美元(+11.0%)、基本金屬及其製品增 0.6 億
美元(+32.5%)、機械增 0.5 億美元(+29.1%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 9 月對美國出口 2,077.8 億美元，較上年同期增 773.1 億美
元(+59.3%)，對中國大陸與香港出口 1,600.9 億美元，增 355.3 億美
元(+28.5%)， 對東協出口1,339.6 億美元 ， 增463.6 億美元(+52.9%)，
對歐洲出口 421.8 億美元 ， 增138.4 億美元(+48.8%)， 對日本出口277.7
億美元，增 58.3 億美元(+26.6%)。

五、主要進口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
9 月自中國大陸與香港進口 135.0 億美元 ， 較上年同月增50.1 億美元
(+59.1%)，自南韓進口 116.3 億美元，增52.6 億美元(+82.5%)，自東
協進口 106.0 億美元，增 52.6 億美元(+98.4%)，自日本進口 64.3 億
美元，增 17.7 億美元(+38.0%)，自歐洲進口 50.2 億美元，增 3.5 億
美元(+7.4%)，自美國進口 48.1 億美元，增 6.5 億美元(+15.5%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 9 月自主要國家/地區進口與上年同期比較 ， 自東協增77.6%、
自南韓增 65.8%、自中國大陸與香港增 42.8%、自日本增 25.7%、自
美國增 21.4%、自歐洲增 10.8%。

六、主要國家/地區貿易概況 
（一）主要國家/地區今年 1-8 月外貿景氣普遍走升，以南韓(1-9 月)出口年
增 56.8%最為強勁，香港、新加坡亦分別年增 49.0%、39.8%；中國
大陸、美國出口分別年增 19.3%及 14.6%，德國(1-7 月)、日本各增
9.7%、8.3%。 
（二）主要國家/地區進口同步上揚，累計 1-8 月以香港進口年增 50.5%較
突出，新加坡、中國大陸分別年增 37.3%、27.0%，南韓(1-9 月)、德
國(1-7 月)分別增 19.7%、10.0%，日本、美國各增 7.8%、4.2%。

### Page 7

7
圖 5 2026 年主要國家/地區出進口年增率
七、綜合分析
（一）受惠於人工智慧(AI)、高效能運算及雲端服務等應用商機持續擴展，
加以步入傳統外銷旺季 、產品價格上漲效應，9 月出口 872.2 億美元，
年增 60.9%，連續 35 個月正成長；進口635.9 億美元，年增51.7%，
主因 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口引申需求擴張，及國際原物料行情居
高；出、進口值及出超 236.3 億美元齊創單月新高。累計 1 至 9 月出
口年增 46.2%、進口增 41.3%。
（二）9 月主要貨品出口普遍較上年同月成長，以資通與視聽產品增 1.0 倍
最佳，包括顯示卡、伺服器、儲存媒體、 交換器及路由器等出口均
呈倍數增加 ；其次為礦產品增 86.4%，主因全球油品供應偏緊支撐價
格維持高檔；電子零組件隨 AI 應用及消費性電子新品備貨需求升溫，
增 45.3%，機械、電機產品亦各增 3 成上下。累計 1 至 9 月資通與視
聽產品、電子零組件出口併計增 59.5%，占總出口比重 79.0%。

（三）主要市場方面，9 月對美國、東協出口值分創歷年單月次高、新高，
年增率各為 1.1 倍及 93.4%，合計對總出口增加貢獻達 7 成；對中國
大陸與香港、歐洲、日本出口各年增 30.2%、20.6%、13.4%。累計 1
至 9 月對五大市場出口值皆為歷年同期最高，其中對美國、東協均
提前刷新全年紀錄 ， 分別年增59.3%、52.9%，對歐洲出口年增48.8%，
對陸港、日本各增 28.5%、26.6%。
（四）展望未來，全球貿易政策及地緣政治情勢變化等，仍增添總體 經濟
不確定性，惟隨人工智慧應用 持續深化，國際雲端服務業者 及各國
加速 AI 基礎建設投資，帶動半導體、伺服器等相關供應鏈需求擴增，
加以科技新品備貨、中國大陸及歐美年底採購旺季將屆， 均有利挹
注我國外銷動能，第 4 季出口可望延續成長態勢。

金融市場投資週報                 2026 年 9 月 30 日
上週重點訊息
◼ 美國企業投資動能維持韌性 ：8 月標普全球美國綜合 PMI 升至 58.4，創2021 年 7 月來高點 ，
製造業、服務業同步擴張至 57 和 58.7； 8 月扣除飛機與國防的核心資本財訂單月增 1.6%，
高於前月修正後的 0.6%。其中，初級金屬、機械及電機設備訂單均呈現成長，顯示企業資本
支出、AI 投資與國防需求仍具韌性，持續支撐美國經濟成長。
◼ 美中同意貿易休兵再延長兩個月：美中同意調降雙邊總值 600 億美元商品的關稅，涵蓋美國

玉米、化妝品及中國家電、玩具等產品，並延長貿易休兵至明年 1 月 10 日，同時中國承諾繼
續供應稀土並擴大採購美國農產品與煤礦，美國則繼續暫停實施 50%穿透性規則；AI 方面，
美中雙方雖未就 AI 監管議題達成共識 ， 但同意建立風險溝通機制 ， 並於11 月底前展開對話 。
◼ Meta 旗下 AI 代理人 Muse 登頂排行榜：Meta 新款個人 AI 代理 Muse 上線後迅速累積逾
250 萬次下載，並登上美國 App Store 下載排行榜首位。相較過去生成式 AI 以問答與內容生
成為主，Muse 可進一步協助安排日程、搜尋資訊、處理文件及完成交易，代表 AI 正從提升
效率工具逐步走向數位勞動力，市場評價焦點也將逐漸由 AI 基礎設施擴散至應用軟體業者。
市場展望
◼ AI 需求持續擴張，科技供應鏈成長動能延續：AI 基礎建設需求持續擴張，帶動 HBM、伺服器
DRAM、 先進製程與封裝等關鍵供應鏈維持成長動能 ， 市場表現由全面上漲逐步轉向結構性分
化。若 AI 伺服器需求持續成長且 CSP 資本支出維持高檔，具供給優勢、定價能力及較高訂單

能見度之企業，其營運表現有機會獲得支撐。整體而言，科技產業獲利表現仍具關注度，惟後
續仍須留意全球景氣、產業供需變化、政策監管及市場評價調整等風險因素。
◼ 債券佈局可關注中天期美國公債及投等債：中天期美國公債及投資級債仍值得持續關注：**雖
然貨幣政策風險下降有助於穩定長期通膨預期 ， 但政府與企業發債供給增加所帶來的排擠效應 ，
以及美國經濟成長展現韌性，仍可能使長天期利率面臨上行壓力。因此，在當前利率環境下，
中天期債券於票息收益與存續期間風險之間相對較為均衡。投資級債方面，彭博美國投資級公
司債指數利差約 80 個基點，雖然利差偏窄，但高基準利率環境仍提供相對較高的收益水準。
因此，在目前市場環境下，中天期美國公債及投資級債仍為值得持續觀察的債券資產類別，惟
後續表現仍須留意利率走勢、信用利差變化及經濟成長動能等因素。

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 澳洲央行決議如預期升息一碼至 4.60%為今年第四度升息，基準利率創 2011 年
以來新高，澳洲央行在政策聲明中表示通膨壓力尚未緩解，不排除在11、12 月
政策會議進一步升息；另一方面，澳洲8 月消費者物價指數(CPI)月增 0.4%略低
於預期，但年增 4.0%仍高於澳洲央行 2~3%的目標區間。
⚫ 美國第二季美 GDP 終值從 1.5%上修至 2.2%， 其中實質個人消費支出創下2025
年 3 月以來最大單月增幅，顯示即使高利率持續，美國家庭消費依然具韌性；
另一方面，美國 8 月核心 PCE 物價指數年增月增 0.2%、3.0%，雙雙低於市場預
期的 0.3%、3.3%，美國 9 月非農就業人數新增 2.9 萬人低於預期的 9 萬人，失
業率由 4.1%上升至 4.2%，8 月非農就業人數亦同步下修至 13.3 萬人，通膨下滑
和勞動力市場略有降溫，降低市場對聯準會 10 月升息預期，Fedwatch 顯示 10

月升息機率下降至 20%左右。
⚫ 受美伊衝突引發的能源價格衝擊影響 ，9 月歐元地區消費者物價調和指數(HICP)
整體上升速度遠超預期，其中，法國年增 3.4%、德國年增 3.3%，歐元區年增
3.8%，均高於歐洲央行的 2%目標，反映能源價格的衝擊持續影響物價，加深歐
洲央行再次升息的壓力。
⚫ 日本上週公佈貨幣政策會議紀要及央行意見摘要顯示多名委員認為當前政策利
率依然處於中性區間之下 ，需警惕通膨上行風險 ， 且未來應該以更快速度升息抑
制通膨，然而意見摘要提及日本經濟財政大臣城內實表示希望央行充分審視過
去升息對景氣之影響 ，市場解讀為政府牽制日本央行未來升息步伐 ， 而財務大臣
片山皋月則重申尊重日本央行的自主性。

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
 美元兌新台幣匯率走勢  
Oct/5/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週台幣匯價高點在 31.760，低點在 31.946，以 31.830 作收，較前一週收盤價
貶值 5 分。 
上週台幣震盪走貶，週初，國際油價持穩於每桶 100 美元上方，市場預期聯準
會後續仍有升息的空間，提振美元指數表現，加以外資賣超台股並匯出，台幣走貶
至 31.900 上方；不過適逢月底也是季底，匯價逢高吸引出口商進場賣美金，緩解台
幣貶值的速度；臨近週末，美國 8 月核心 PCE 數據 3%低於預期 3.3%，但市場等待
9 月非農就業數據，交投偏向謹慎觀望，最終在央行調節之下收在 31.830。

＜未來分析＞ 
    預估本週台幣區間震盪。就國際市場而言，美國 9 月非農就業人數新增 2.9 萬
人，低於市場預期 9 萬人，且失業率由 4.1%上升至 4.2%，顯示就業市場動能略有
減弱，市場對於 10 月升息的預期下降，但仍預期 12 月會再度升息。美元指數雖然
脫離高點，但仍在 102 附近高位震盪，預計將給台幣帶來壓力；就台灣市場來看，
台股再創收盤新高且外資轉為買超台股，且隨著 AI 需求持續帶動台灣經濟出口表
現強勁，預計出口商後續拋匯需求將持續提供台幣支撐。綜合以上，預估本週台幣
區間震盪。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.600-32.000

美元兌人民幣匯率走勢  
Oct/5/2026 
＜上週走勢＞ 
上週人民幣震盪偏升。隨著美中兩國完成新一輪談判，雙方同意延長休兵，從
原定的 11 月延長到明年 1 月 ， 降低短期貿易摩擦升溫的風險 ， 支撐人民幣的走勢由
6.7170 走升至 6.7030；不過由於美伊戰事持續 ，國際油價持穩於每桶 100 美元上方 ，
加以美元指數及美債收益率雙雙走高 ， 令人民幣由升轉貶至6.7200 上方 ； 臨近週末，
美國公布 9 月非農數據降溫，聯準會10 月升息預期下滑，美元指數回落，境外人民
幣走升至 6.7000 週內高點，終場境外人民幣收在 6.7061，週線升值 166 點，境內人
民幣於十一長假前夕收盤於在 6.7053，週線升值 72 點。

＜未來分析＞ 
    預估本週人民幣區間震盪 。 就國際市場來看 ， 美國 9 月非農就業人數新增 2.9 萬
人，遠低於市場預期 9 萬人，且失業率由 4.1%上升至 4.2%，顯示就業市場動能略
為減弱。數據公布後，市場對於聯準會10 月升息的預期下降，美元指數小幅回落，
有助於人民幣的表現；就中國國內市場來看，人行中間價格再度升破 6.7400，有助
於人民幣的走勢 ， 不過10/1~10/7 是中國十一長假 ， 預計人民幣走勢主要將受國際美
元影響；最後就技術面來看，USD/CNH 日線圖中，各天期均線仍向下發散，空頭格
局不變，不過匯價反彈至 20 日均線附近，顯示下行動能暫緩。綜合以上，預估本週
人民幣區間震盪。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6900-6.7300

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週 USD/JPY 震盪上行。週初，美元指數持穩在 101 以上高位，USD/JPY 大致
在 156.50 至 157.85 間波動；週間，適逢日本上半年會計年度結算，出口商拋匯使資
金匯回力道強勁 ， 帶動USD/JPY 下行最低來到 156.35，但逢低有買盤進場，USD/JPY
回到 157 整數關卡之上，後隨美債殖利率創高、美元指數突破 102 關卡，USD/JPY
上行最高來至 158.45；臨近週末，美國公布 9 月非農就業人數低於預期，USD/JPY
一度回落至 157 下方，終場收在 157.83，週線上漲 57 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 USD/JPY 區間震盪 ， 主要原因如下 ： 一 、日本 9 月製造業 PMI 由 54.9
回落至 54.1，增長速度放慢至六個月以來的低位，主因為產出和新訂單增長放緩，
經濟復甦力道可能減緩，不利日圓表現。二、美國 8 月核心 PCE 物價指數年增 3.0%，
美國 9 月非農數據 2.9 萬人均低於預期，通膨和勞動力市場略有降溫使市場對聯準
會升息預期下滑，加之部分聯準會官員表示無需急於進一步升息，Fedwatch 顯示 10
月升息機率降至兩成，然而美國第二季 GDP 上修至 2.2%經濟基本面穩健仍將支撐
美元。三、就技術面而言，日線圖中，USD/JPY 匯價處於區間波動，且各天期均線
趨平，上方 158.50 有年線、季線壓力，下方 156.25 則有月線支撐，預期短線以區間
震盪可能性較高。綜合以上，預估 USD/JPY 本週呈現區間震盪。

＜上週走勢＞ 
  上週 EUR/USD 下跌。週初，美元指數持穩在 101 以上高位，EUR/USD 震盪下
行至 1.1310，而在紐約聯儲主席威廉姆斯發表不急於再次升息的偏鴿言論後，
EUR/USD 反彈至 1.1340；週間，美國 8 月 PCE 數據低於預期，EUR/USD 一度反彈
至 1.1380，然而在全球債券拋售潮下，資金回流美元帶動美元指數突破 102 關卡，
EUR/USD 跌破 1.1300 關卡最低來至 1.1214；臨近週末，美國 9 月非農就業人數低
於預期，EUR/USD 反彈至 1.1285 後回落，終場收在 1.1253，週線下跌 138 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
預估本週 EUR/USD 震盪偏弱，主要原因如下：一、上週公佈歐元地區消費者
物價調和指數(HICP)，法國年增 3.4%、德國年增 3.3%、歐元區年增 3.8%，顯示能
源價格衝擊依然使通膨壓力居高不下， 市場預期歐洲央行可能在 12 月再度升息 ， 將
帶給歐元部分支撐。二、美國 8 月核心 PCE 物價指數年增 3.0%、月增 0.2%低於預
期，而 9 月非農就業人數 2.9 萬人低於預期的 9 萬人 ， 失業率則由 4.1%上升至 4.2%，
就業降溫及通膨回落降低市場對聯準會十月升息的預期，然而第二季美國 GDP 終
值從 1.5%上修至 2.2%，經濟基本面持穩仍有利美元維持強勢。三、技術面而言，在
日線圖中，EUR/USD 匯價在各天期均線下方並跌破 1.1300 整數關卡，月線向下穿
過季線形成死亡交叉，顯示短線空方動能較強。綜合以上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈
現震盪偏弱的可能性較高。

## Document 114: 9 月会合以降に日銀を取り巻く状況が変わった印象

Source PDF: all report pdf/9 月会合以降に日銀を取り巻く状況が変わった印象.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

2026 年 10 月 7 日(水)

1/3 
9 月会合以降に日銀を取り巻く状況が変わった印象  
 経済調査部主席エコノミスト藤代宏一 
（℡：050-5474-6123）

・時事通信は10月６日付で「日銀が『基調的な物価上昇率』について、２％程度に到達したと
判断する方向で検討に入ることが５日、分かった」と報じた。記事内には10月29・30日開催
の金融政策決定会合で取りまとめる展望レポートで、そうした認識が記述されるとしてい
る。

・筆者はこの報道が現実になる可能性が高いと判断している。日銀は予てから基調的物価上昇
率が２％に到達する時期を「2026年度後半から2027年度」と予想してきたので（2026年度後
半にあたる）10月にそうした認識を示さないと、ハト派的な印象を与えてしまうことにな
る。

・既に日銀の政策態度は基調的物価上昇率を２％に「引き上げる」段階から、２％以上に「上
振れさせない」段階に移行している。局面が変化した理由としては、やはり原油高とそれに
対する企業の価格設定行動の積極化が大きいだろう。従来、原油高は企業収益を圧迫するこ
とで、賃上げを阻害し、個人消費の停滞に繋がってきた。そうした環境下で内需は拡大しな
いため、原油高は短期的にインフレ要因となっても、やや長い目でみるとデフレ的な色彩を
帯びていた。もっとも、2026年春先以降の原油高局面では、政府の手厚い支援もあり、企業
・日経平均株価は先行き12 ヶ月72,000 円程度で推移するだろう 
・USD/JPY は先行き12 ヶ月 155 円程度で推移するだろう 
・日銀は政策金利を12 月に 1.5％とした後、27 年末までに2.25％とするだろう 
・FED はFF 金利を、年内は4.0％で据え置くだろう

### Page 2

2/3 
は価格転嫁を積極的に進めた。企業物価指数は４月に前月比＋2.8％と即座に上昇し、前年比
伸び率は８月時点でも＋7.6％と高止まりしている。日銀短観（６月および９月調査）では１
年後の販売価格見通しが急伸した。こうした早期かつ大幅な価格転嫁の進展によって、企業
収益は傷みにくくなっている。このことは、名目賃金と名目消費が伸びやすい構図にあるこ
とを浮き彫りにしている。

・日銀が、基調的物価上昇率が２％に到達したとの認識を示すのであれば、それは利上げペー
スの加速を自ら正当化することになる。さすがに10月の利上げはビハインド・ザ・カーブに
陥ったことを認めたと市場参加者に受け止められる恐れがあるため、見送られると予想する
が、（前回利上げから約３ヶ月が経過する）12月会合では利上げが決定されるだろう。そう
なれば、３～４ヶ月に一度の利上げが標準ペースとなることが市場参加者に広く意識される
だろう。

・利上げを巡っては、経済指標に加えて「政府との距離感」を考慮する必要があり、予想は難
儀であった。しかしながら、ここ数週間で情勢は変化したように感じる。９月25日に片山さ
つき財務相が「官邸とも相談の上、私から初めて申し上げるが、先日の日米首脳会談では、
為替について、トランプ大統領から円安への懸念が示された」と発言したことを皮切りに、
同日には城内経済再生相が「アベノミクス的な意味でのリフレーション政策は、もうそうい
う局面は終わっている」とした。また10月５日に公表された９月30日の経済財政諮問会議の
議事要旨では、民間議員である若田部昌澄氏が「デフレではない状況になりつつある現状で
リフレ政策が終焉を迎えているというのは当然のことだ」との見解を示し、さらに９月の金
融政策決定会合で反対票を投じた佐藤綾野日銀政策委員は６日までに共同通信の単独インタ

### Page 3

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
3/3 
ビューに応じて「（政策金利を）段階的に調整していく方針には賛成している」とした。９
月18日会合における「主な意見」で内閣府の出席者（おそらく城内大臣）が利上げに消極的
と読める発言をしていたことと整合しないが、それは9月18日から25日の間に政府の態度が変
化した可能性が指摘できる。

・10月会合までに円安が進む、あるいは原油高等の要因によってＦｅｄが利上げに踏み切る事
態となれば、日銀も追随する可能性があるものの、そうした状況に至らなければ、10月は
「既往の利上げ効果を見極めたい」などとして利上げを見送るだろう。

・なお、これらに鑑みて、筆者は2027年７月の審議委員人事が中立ないしはタカ派的な人選に
なると予想する。高田委員と田村委員に考え方の近い人物に白羽の矢が立ち、リフレ派が送
り込まれることはないと考えている。利上げに積極的な２名の後任人事にリフレ派と目され
る人物が浮上した場合、金融市場では長期金利上昇や円安の反応が予想される。

## Document 128: [000660] SK하이닉스(000660) 소음보다 방향성에SK하이닉스(000660) 소음보다 방향성에소음보다 방향성에 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[000660] SK하이닉스(000660) 소음보다 방향성에SK하이닉스(000660) 소음보다 방향성에소음보다 방향성에 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

기업분석 2025.09.28 www.daishin.com

Issue & News 
SK하이닉스  
(000660) 
 소음보다 방향성에 
류형근

hyungkeun.ryu@daishin.com

투자의견 BUY  
매수, 유지 
6개월 목표주가 3,200,000 
유지  
현재주가 
(26.09.23) 
1,862,000 
반도체업종

◼ 최근 시장은 2가지 Risk를 제기. 다만, 과도한 우려는 기우. 
◼ 현 주가는 보다 강인해지는 Fundamental의 가치를 미반영한 수준. 
◼ 비중 확대 전략 유효. 목표주가와 매수의견 유지.

투자전략: 업황을 대변하지 못하고 있는 주가 
목표주가와 매수의견 유지. 현 주가는 업황의 강세를 충분히 반영하지 못하
고 있다고 생각. 1) 메모리반도체 사이클의 추가 확장 (구매 경쟁 심화에 따른 
단기 가격 상승 폭 확대, 기대치를 상회할 4Q26 DRAM 판매량), 2) 3Q26 실
적발표에서 보다 구체화될 주주환원 정책의 방향성에 보다 집중할 필요. 
시장에서 제기하는 Risk가 있다면, 2가지. 1) 주요 고객사향 HBM4 품질 이슈, 
2) Solidigm IPO와 해외 공장 투자에 따른 경계심리 확대가 그 대상. 당사는 
해당 Risk가 Fundamental을 저해할 것으로 보고 있지 않으며, 단기 노이즈보
다는 사이클 확장의 선순환에 보다 주목할 필요가 있다고 생각. 
1. HBM4 품질 이슈, 과도한 우려는 기우 
3Q26부터 HBM4 출하가 본격화될 것이라는 기존 입장을 유지. HBM4 비중
은 3Q26 전체 판매량의 40%, 2027년 연간 50% 중반까지 상승할 것. 
일각에서는 주요 고객사향 HBM4 품질 이슈로 사업 성과가 기대치를 하회할 
수 있다는 우려를 표출. 다만, 과도한 우려는 기우. 후공정 단에서 문제가 일
부 발생했을 수 있으나, 해결 불가능한 Risk라 보기 어렵고, 불량에 따른 배
상금을 논할 단계도 아니라는 판단. 
2. 해외 공장 투자, 실보다 득이 크다 
언론보도에 따르면, SK하이닉스는 해외 공장 투자와 Solidigm IPO를 추진 중. 
해당 결정이 현실화될 경우, 시장은 단기적으로 이를 부정적으로 받아들일 
가능성이 존재. 다만, 결과적으로, 실보다 득이 큰 선택이 될 것이라 생각. 
- 해외 투자가 현실화되더라도, 실질 생산 기여 시점은 2028년 이후일 가능
성이 높음. 미국의 건설 경기와 공장 구축의 세부 단계 등을 감안할 필요. 
- 해외 투자 결정이 관세 Risk를 축소시킬 수 있는 변화라는 점도 감안할 필
요. “불확실성 해소”는 Upcycle의 중반부에서 반도체 주식에 대한 좋은 투자
포인트가 되어왔고, 이번에도 다르지 않을 것이라는 판단.

VIETNAM 
HAS 
CONTINUE
D 
TO ENJOY VERY STRONG ECONOMIC GROWTH
, 
DESPITE EXTERNAL SHOCKS 
Vietnam’s economic success story has continued
 , 
despite last year’s US tariff shock and this year’s energy shock  
◼ 
 Economic growth accelerated to 8% in 2025 and 8.2% y/y in H1 2026, driven by exports, investment and private consumption. 
 It is 
projected to slow in the short term, reaching a still strong 
 level of 
 7.3% in 2026. 
 External accounts strengthened in 
 2025
, but 
they 
have 
 deteriorat
 ed 
in recent months. Public and external debt ratios 
 remain  
moderate.  
◼ 
 Vietnam is highly exposed to the global energy shock
 , 
but 
the consequences have been 
 less severe  
than expected  
so far
 . Economic 
activity 
 has continued to grow 
 strong
 ly
, notably because the export
 -
 oriented manufacturing sector 
 has been 
 boosted by global 
demand for AI goods. 
 F
iscal and current account balances can absorb a deterioration
 . 
 I
nflation has increased 
 while 
 currency 
depreciation pressures  
have remained 
 manageable. 
 Yet, 
as 
 the negative impact of high energy prices 
 is expected to persist, 
 V
ietnam  
could face 
 more 
severe external  
liquidity tensions going forward.

Vietnam’s export base has strengthened further over the past year, notwithstanding the risin
 g 
US tariffs
 , notably 
because 
 Vietnam 
 has been a  
key beneficiar
 y 
of the surgin
 g 
global demand for AI
 -
 related goods  
◼ 
 Export growth strengthened in 2025 (+17% in value), driven by electronic goods (+48%) and sales to the U
 nited 
S
tates 
(+28%). V
ietnam 
was 
 one of 
the main targets of President Trump’s tariff war in early 2025, but the tariff shock was much smaller than initially feared, 
and exports were boosted by surgin
 g 
AI
-
 related demand. V
 ietnam  
increased its global market share (1.9% of world exports 
 in 2025 
vs. 1.7% in 2024), supported by gains in the US market (5.7% of US imports vs. 4.2% in 2024) and the semiconductor sector (
 Vietnam 
is estimated  
to 
account for  
2.9% of world exports of semiconductors vs. 1.6% in 2024).  
◼ 
 Solid growth dynamics in industrial 
 out
p
ut 
and exports ha
 ve 
continued 
 in 2026
 . Exports rose by 
 21.9% y/y in the first eight months 
of the year
 , 
led by the strong increase in both volume and price of tech goods.  
However, 
 Vietnam  
adds little value to the tech goods  
that 
it exports as it speciali
 s
es mostly in assembl
 ing 
and packaging chips to form electronic components. 
 Therefore, i
 ts balance of 
trade of semiconductor goods is negative
 , as i
 mports and exports of tech goods are increasing in tandem.  
◼ 
 V
ietnam
 ’s trade surplus with the U
 nited 
 S
tates 
has 
 surged in the past three years and 
 recently  
bec
a
me the largest bilateral surplus, 
ahead of Mexico and China
 . 
It 
 reach
 ed 
 USD2
 19 
 bn in the 12
 -
month period to 
 July 
 2026
, up 
from USD116 bn in 2022  
(
vs. 
 Mexico’s 
trade surplus with the 
 US: 
USD213 bn  
and China’s trade surplus with the US: 
 USD165 bn  
during  
the same period
 ).  
◼ 
 Meanwhile, V
 ietnam
 ’s imports of Chinese goods have increased rapidly (+3
 6.9
% y/y in 
 the first eight months of 
 2026), causing its 
trade deficit with China to surge.  
Indeed, V
 ietnam  
has played a growing role in the rerouting of Chinese goods to the US. It has also 
benefited largely from the reorgani
 s
ation of production chains in the region and continued to attract 
 significant  
 FDI, mostly from 
Asian countries. Net FDI inflows have exceeded 4% of GDP in recent years, which has fostered continued expansion of the 
manufacturing sector and progress up the value chain.  
◼ 
 In the 
 short term
 , 
solid export growth dynamics are likely to continue, assuming 
 that 
 there is no correction in the AI cycle and 
 that 
manufacturing production 
 is 
 not hindered by energy supply difficulties.  
◼ 
 US trade policy remains 
 a source of 
 uncertain
 ty
. The US effective tariff rate on V
 ietnam’s  
goods, which rose from 3.9% at end
 -
 2024 
to 19.3% in Aug
 ust 
 2025, 
 has fallen 
 back 
 in 2026 (
 as the US Supreme Court cancelled ‘reciprocal’ tariffs)
 ; it is now close to 10
 %. This 
is higher than 
 some South Asian 
 neighbours
 , 
 but not high enough to undermine the competitiveness of 
 Vietnamese 
 goods 
substantially
 . However, Vietnam is 
 still 
 exposed to the risk of new US tariffs, given its connector role between China 
 and 
 the U
 nited 
S
tates 
and its huge trade surplus with the U
 nited 
 S
tates
.

Vietnam  
is a net energy importer highly exposed to the consequences of the conflict in the Middle East. So far, 
the impact of hydrocarbon supply disruptions has been 
 more moderate  
than expected, but it could 
 worsen 
 in 
the coming months.  
◼ 
 Vietnam

## Document 228: Fiscal update_ States learning the laws of compound interest (2026-10-10)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Fiscal update_ States learning the laws of compound interest (2026-10-10).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 19
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#fiscalpolicy #australia #aud #inflation #federalbudget
Fiscal update: States learning the laws of compound interest
By Pat Bustamante
Senior Economist, Westpac
16:33 September 23 2026 Share
State budgets remain under pressure. Higher borrowing costs, changes to NDIS funding
arrangements and a sharper-than-expected housing downturn are weighing on state
finances. Offsetting this to some extent, stronger consumer spending and firmer economic
activity are providing a modest revenue boost.

### Page 2

As we previously foreshadowed, a deeper-than-expected downturn in house prices and
turnover, coupled with ongoing above-average construction costs, are set to delay the expected
recovery in the combined state fiscal position. Combined with the global sell-off in sovereign
State budgets are set to deteriorate more than previously expected. The global bond sell-off has
pushed borrowing costs higher, while changes to NDIS funding arrangements are shifting costs to

the states. At the same time, the housing market downturn has been faster and broader than
anticipated, weighing on stamp duty revenue. A firmer household sector and stronger economic
activity will provide some offset. Improved GST revenues will provide some support to state finances
but getting the distribution right could be tricky and there may be a case for additional ‘top up’
payments from the Federal government over and above GST allocations.

Higher bond yields are expected to add around $3.5bn to state interest costs over the next four
years, while weaker housing activity is expected to reduce revenue by around $8-9 billion in FY2027
alone (some of which is already factored in to state budgets). Stronger consumption and a slightly
more resilient labour market are expected to provide a partial offset of around $1.5bn over the next
four years.
The deterioration in the combined state fiscal position of $13.5bn over the next four years will delay

the expected recovery in state finances. It will also push state interest payments to a record high of
$37bn (1.1% of nominal GDP) by FY2030 and lift real (or inflation adjusted) net debt per capita to a
new record level.
Risks to the outlook appear broadly balanced. The conflict in the Middle East presents a downside
risk through higher energy prices and weaker confidence. Offsetting this is the potential for an even
larger investment upswing, driven by data centres and associated energy infrastructure.

### Page 3

bond markets, which has seen yields rise by an average of 60bps since state budgets were
published, we expect the combined state cash position to deteriorate by a further $13.5bn
compared with published 2026 budgets, mainly impacting FY2027. As a result, combined state
interest repayments are expected to increase to a record 1.1% of GDP by FY2030, while net debt
per person will also reach a record high across several states. Firmer economic conditions
provide a partial offset, mainly through higher GST revenue, underscoring the importance of
getting the GST distribution right.

State budgets continue to come under pressure. Higher yields, higher service delivery costs
(particularly construction costs affecting a still-elevated pipeline of projects), the need to deliver more
services for a growing population, and changes in program funding arrangements (most notably the
NDIS) are increasing the cost of doing business for state governments. Meanwhile, the sharper-than-
expected housing downturn is hitting both actual and expected stamp duty revenues (typically the
second-largest source of state government revenue), while the gradual easing in the labour market is
also seeing slower growth in payroll tax receipts. Since our last update, the main changes impacting
state budgets include: higher yields; changing funding arrangements for the NDIS; a sharper-than-
expected housing downturn; and partly offsetting this, firmer consumption and economic activity.

### Page 4

Bond sell-off leaves states paying more
As a result of rising energy prices due to the ongoing conflict in the Middle East, and large fiscal deficits
particularly in the US, there has been a sell-off in sovereign bonds. At the same time, the IT sector has
ramped up issuance of high-quality, higher-yielding investment grade bonds, both overseas and in
Australia. The result has been a broad global rise in bond yields. Across the Australian states, 10-

year bond yields are up around 60bps compared to when states published their 2026-27
budgets.
For states, many of whom have built up large stocks of debt to fund much-needed infrastructure, higher
interest rates directly increase debt-servicing costs. We estimate that interest expenses across the
states will rise by $3.5bn over the four years to FY2030, reaching 1.2% of nominal GDP by FY2030, a
record high.
This estimate takes into account the projected rollover of state debt. If all state debt had to be rolled

over immediately at current yields, interest expenses would increase by around $5bn in a single
year and by around $20bn over four years.
While much of this reflects factors outside the control of state governments, they are not powerless.
The 10-year semis bond spread over AGS ranges from 50 to 80bps across the states, in part reflecting
differences in fiscal positions.

### Page 5

NDIS reforms shift costs and risks to the states
NDIS reforms are expected to deliver significant savings to the Commonwealth, reaching almost 1% of
GDP in the medium term according to the 2026 IGR. However, under the reforms, states will take on
greater responsibility for funding and delivering services for certain groups, beginning with the ‘Thriving
Kids’ program for children with developmental delay and low to moderate support needs. While some

states have already budgeted for these measures, the associated costs are not yet fully reflected
across all state budgets. Further fiscal pressures could emerge if states are required to support
additional cohorts, beyond ‘Thriving Kids’, in the future.
With spending growth assumed to slow sharply from here, the transfer of additional responsibilities
from the Commonwealth to the states suggests the risks to state expenditure remain tilted to the

upside, and budget outcomes to the downside.
Housing downturn cuts state revenues by almost $10bn in FY2027
The downturn in established house prices has been both faster and steeper than initially expected. As
a result, we have revised our house price forecasts lower, reflecting both recent weakness and our
updated interest rate profile. We now expect house prices to fall by around 6% in 2026, resulting in a
peak-to-trough decline of just over 7%. The adjustment is expected to be somewhat larger in Sydney

and Melbourne, where prices are projected to decline by closer to 10%. Turnover is also expected to
record a bigger decline of 24% vs our initial assessment of a 19% fall.

### Page 6

This will weigh on stamp duty revenue, which is typically the second-largest source of state
government revenue, after GST distributions and other Commonwealth transfers. Overall, we
estimate the housing downturn will reduce state revenues by around $9bn in FY2027.
While some of this downside has already been factored into state budgets, jurisdictions that released
budgets before the Federal Budget, including Vic and WA, have yet to fully capture the deterioration in

housing market conditions. Our updated 2026 budget estimates incorporate this downside.
While much of that is a cyclical swing that will partly reverse as an easing in interest rates comes
through in 2028, the tax policy changes mean investor activity is likely to be permanently lower as well.
Our estimates suggest that average annual growth housing turnover could be around 1ppt lower going
forward as a result of weaker investor activity. At current levels, 1% equates to around $400m a year –

not material in any single year, but increasingly significant when compounded over the medium to
longer term.
Firmer economic activity providing some support
On the other side, broader economic activity has held up better than expected. The data centre and
renewable investment surge is supporting GDP, consumption and employment growth, prompting
upgrades to our real economy forecasts. Overall, this should offset around $1.5bn over the next four
years, mainly due to stronger GST receipts, with the firmer labour market also supporting state payroll

tax collections.
Putting it all together
We continue to expect the fiscal impulse to remain mildly supportive in some states, although it is well
below the levels seen in recent years. SA, NSW, WA and Vic continue to receive some support from
state fiscal policy settings (ex-Commonwealth), while the impulse elsewhere is broadly neutral. State
elections due later this year in Vic (November 2026) and next year in NSW (March 2027) may see
some additional fiscal support at the margin in the form of ‘election sweetener’ policies.

### Page 7

Importantly, the public infrastructure cycle has now moved past its peak across most states. While
project pipelines remain elevated in level terms, growth is slowing, particularly in NSW and Vic. Qld
remains the key exception, with infrastructure spending continuing to ramp up ahead of the Brisbane
2032 Olympics.

### Page 8

Despite this, debt continues to accumulate. The combination of higher borrowing requirements and
elevated interest rates is driving a sharp increase in net interest payments, adding to fiscal pressures
over the medium term and reducing budget flexibility.

### Page 9

State interest payments are projected to reach a record $37bn (1.1% of nominal GDP) by FY2030.

### Page 10

Real (or inflation adjusted) net debt per capita is set to reach a record high by the end of the estimates
period (FY2030). However, there are significant differences across the states, with real (or inflation
adjusted) net debt per capita varying considerably by jurisdiction.

### Page 11

Bottom line
Historically governments would keep debt at a lower level relative to state product by periodically
engaging in fiscal consolidation. However, this is much easier to do when economies are posting
robust growth. With growth on the soft side, cost of living issues persisting and additional calls on the
state government purse to supply some fast-growing areas of welfare support the ‘window of
opportunity’ for getting ahead of these fiscal pressures looks significantly smaller.

## Document 250: Global Strategy Views_ Competition for Capital

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Strategy Views_ Competition for Capital.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1. There are two themes dominating our investor conversations: the impact of AI and 
the rise in interest rates. The two issues are linked as demand for capital from both 
the private and public sectors increase. In the private sector a surge in capex 
spending to fund AI infrastructure has eaten into free cash flow and forced 
companies to raise more in debt and equity markets. Meanwhile, government 
borrowing needs have increased as priorities shift towards upgrading critical 
infrastructure, energy security and defense at a time when cyclical inflationary 
pressures driven by higher energy prices are also resulting in higher policy rates. The 
combination has pushed up the cost of capital. As recently as 2022, for example, 
30-year bond yields in Germany and Japan were close to zero (Exhibit 1). A rise in 
yields, together with more uncertainty (over geopolitics and the future impact of AI) 
have, in combination, pushed up the cost of capital.

2. This rise in yields comes after a near-record period of equity outperformance 
relative to bonds over 10-year holding periods (Exhibit 2).

Exhibit 1: Rising yields and uncertainty have increased the cost of capital 
30-year government bond benchmark yields (%) 
                                
   
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
       
     
  

### Page 2

As equities have continued to outperform bonds, equity risk premia have fallen back to 
levels last seen in the late 1990s, leaving equity markets more vulnerable to further 
increases in bond yields. That said, in the US and Japan, the ERP has bounced off recent 
lows (Exhibit 3).

3. The impact of rising bond yields, however, depends not only on the level of yields, but 
also on the reasons as well as the speed of the adjustment (Exhibit 4). It is the speed 
issue that has caused the biggest impact in the recent past. As our US strategists note, 
stocks have usually generated positive returns alongside rising interest rates unless the 
pace of rising rates exceeded 2+ standard deviations and this is a pattern that is broadly

4. But despite the rise in yields, nominal GDP and profit growth remain strong and, until 
recently, the impact of rising bond yields has been offset by the strength in corporate 
profits. Earnings growth has been the main driver of equity returns over the past 18 
months in all regions (Exhibit 5). This strength has meant that PE multiples have either 
been flat (Japan and Europe) or have fallen (the US, Asia and EM).

the start of the year to 19x, in line with its long-run average, despite the market being

5. While US equities have driven the bulk of equity outperformance for much of the 
fifteen years post the financial crisis, the improvement in earnings across regions, and 
the sharper de-rating in the US, has resulted in equity performance being 
broader-based by geography. Since 2025, the US equity market has been the weakest of 
the major regions (Exhibit 6), a turnaround from the dominant trend since the financial 
crisis and increasing investor returns from a more geographically diversified portfolio.

6. Supportive profit growth has been accompanied by positive earnings revisions, with 
2026 and 2027 estimates moving higher across major regions. There are four broad 
areas which have been driving much of this profit growth. First, technology earnings 
continue to be very robust. Second, rising energy prices have pushed up profits in the 
commodity sector. Third, banks have generally enjoyed strong earnings backed by 
positive nominal GDP growth, steep yield curves and strong private sector balance 
sheets. Finally, sectors such as Industrials have benefited from the surge in AI capex 
spending which has spilled over into improved revenues for the ‘pick and shovels’ of AI 
infrastructure. The breadth of the earnings’ growth has also increased the opportunity 
for investors to diversify across sectors as well as countries.

7. While technology profit growth has remained strong, the surge in capex spending 
among the hyperscalers has increasingly eaten through their free cash flow (Exhibit 8) 
prompting companies to look for alternative sources of funding, in the credit and equity 
markets.  The capex spending growth for AA-rated issuers has been substantial: the 65% 
year-over-year growth in Q2 marks the 10th consecutive quarter that aggregate AA 
capex growth exceeds 35%. They have also turned to the convertible bond market 
where volume has also increased year to date reaching $135 billion in the US, with 
AI-related borrowers driving 44% of total issuance, on our estimates. 
Our Credit team raised their full-year US IG gross issuance forecast by $200bn to $2.3 
trillion and AI-related issuers account for a quarter of USD IG gross supply this year, 
pushing overall supply to a year-to-date record.

### Page 7

surge. Globally technology has seen its PE ratio moderate, falling below its 20-year 
median (Exhibit 10). Meanwhile, the spending of these companies has spilled over into 
better earnings and more visible cash flows in many other parts of the economy. 
Healthcare, Consumer Discretionary and Industrials are all more expensive in absolute 
terms than IT, and Industrials has re-rated and now has a PE close to the 90th percentile 
of its 20-year range.

9. For these reasons, we have argued that Technology equities have not experienced a 
classic bubble because valuation levels are much lower than in previous financial 
bubbles. Nevertheless, the risk for investors is less about valuation and more whether 
there is an unsustainable earnings bubble. Bubbles in earnings, rather than valuations, 
have occurred in previous periods.  The Bank sector, for example, briefly became the 
biggest sector in the S&P 500 in the run up to the financial crisis of 2008/09 (Exhibit 11). 
Unlike the technology bubble of the late 1990s, or the Japanese bubble of the late 
1980s, Bank stocks did not experience a major valuation bubble at the time, but the 
surge in earnings that drove the sector’s outperformance proved to be unsustainable.

### Page 8

There are, however, important differences to the Tech sector today. The earnings of the 
banks were boosted by sharply rising leverage and the asset that they were financing – 
US real estate – did experience a valuation bubble. When the housing market collapsed, 
pushing economies into recession, it triggered a sharp fall in underlying earnings for the 
banking sector. There are important differences today. First, technology profits remain 
very robust, and balance sheets are strong overall. Second, interest coverage ratios for 
the aggregate S&P 500 and its median stock, for example, rank in the 99th and 68th 
percentiles relative to the past 20 years. Third, demand for compute is accelerating and 
outstripping supply; Microsoft recently declared their intent to triple data centre 
capacity in 6 years. The bottlenecks are increasingly power, land and data centre 
capacity rather than a lack of compute demand. At our annual Communacopia 
Technology Conference, Nvidia reiterated its 2030 AI TAM outlook to $3tn-$4tn and 
supply constraints continue to be broad based. Nonetheless, any slowdown in profit 
growth, in an environment of a much higher cost of capital, could put downward 
pressure on equity prices, reducing confidence in future cash flows across the ecosystem 
from the hyperscalers to the ‘pick and shovels’ that have been benefiting from the capex 
boom.  
10. Given the near-term risks in bond markets, we remain neutral on equities, alongside 
other asset classes on a 3-month horizon, but continue to be overweight equities over 
12 months. While profit growth is likely to slow from the recent strong pace, ongoing 
growth in nominal GDP should underpin equity markets and the earnings cycle 
propelling equity returns even without any valuation expansion from current levels and 
our economists continue to see a low risk of recession. As our asset allocation team has 
shown, earnings growth and ROE are likely to continue to be the key drivers of equity 
performance relative to bonds, and we expect these fundamentals to remain supportive 
for equity returns. Nevertheless, we continue to recommend well diversified equity 
exposure as opportunities continue to broaden across geographies, sectors and factors.

Stock correlations are at multi-year lows across the major equity markets (Exhibit 12).

Even within sectors like technology, correlations have fallen sharply (Exhibit 13). The 
‘magnificent seven’ that was long seen as a homogeneous group of technology winners 
has also seen significant dispersion of returns. Differentiation, diversification and alpha 
generation are likely to remain the key drivers of equity returns. With higher bond yields, 
valuations are not likely to rise making earnings the critical driver of returns for investors.

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
TODAY’S INSIGHTS
Financial Markets
• Share markets generally stalled across the globe. In the US,
stocks partially retraced gains from earlier in the week, with
the S&P 500 (–0.2%), Dow Jones (–0.7%) and NASDAQ
(–0.2%) all declining. Stocks fared worse across the pond,

with the Euro Stoxx 50 effectively unwinding all gains in
recent days (–1.5%), marked by chunky declines in Germany’s
DAX (–1.4%) and France’s CAC (–1.2%), alongside a larger
decline in London’s FTSE 100 (–0.8%). Stocks were also
weaker across much of Asia, falling in Tokyo (–0.9%), Hong
Kong (–0.6%) and Seoul (–2.0%). The ASX 200 only edged
slightly lower, down –0.1%, but futures markets are pointing
to a weaker open this morning.
•  The treasury yield curve steepened overnight. Gains in the

shorter-end saw 2-year yields fall 3bps to 4.77% – this was
despite a strong lift in consumer inflation expectations and
a hawkish FOMC Minutes, with market pricing for further
rate hikes from the FOMC instead softening at the margin.
Meanwhile, elevated yields at the long end drew very strong
demand at the latest 10-year auction, stabilising 10-year
yields at around 5.28%.
•  Bonds were more varied elsewhere, with Bunds bull
steepening amid a steady long-end, while Gilts bear

steepened amid a 6bp lift in the 10-year. ACGBs were
broadly steady during local trading, with yields inching only
slightly higher, though that gave way to gains in futures
trading overnight. Swaps markets continue to have around
6-7bps priced in for the RBA’s November meeting, while a
rate hike in December is seen at closer to 50/50 odds.
•  The USD bounced back overnight as the DXY rallied to an
intraday high of 102.5 before settling 0.4% higher at around

102.3. The Euro led the way lower, falling 0.6% versus the
greenback to just under USD1.12, back around its recent
lows. The Sterling shared a similar fate, down 0.5% toward
the USD1.321 level, remaining largely rangebound between
USD1.32-1.33 since late September. The Yen bucked the
wider trend, holding broadly steady. The Aussie dollar
meanwhile fell 0.3% to around USD0.696, unwinding gains
from earlier in the week.
•  Crude oil prices remain elevated as markets try to evaluate

the net impact of opposing forces – signs of recovering flows
of oil out of the Middle East is a positive, but reports that
Iran has increased the number of attacks on ships transiting
the Strait of Hormuz has kept a floor under prices. Brent was
up 0.5% to US$101.1/bbl overnight, still unable to break
sustainably under the US$100/bbl mark as of yet. Copper
prices lifted 0.4% to US$14,476 on the LME, gains being
driven by risks of tighter supply amid strikes at a major

Chilean mine, though the turn in risk sentiment overnight and
firmer USD capped gains. Gold prices meanwhile fell 1.3% to
around US$4,111/oz.
International Data
In the US, the FOMC’s Minutes  from the September meeting
reinforced the hawkish sentiment present at the time of the
decision to raise the fed funds rate by 25bps to a midpoint
of 3.875%. “All” participants supported the decision to raise
rates in September, and “most” saw further tightening by

year-end as appropriate. Underscoring that view was the fact
that “almost all” participants saw inflation risks as tilted to the
upside. Importantly, the assessment of the inflation outlook at
the time had already anticipated a 0.2ppt downward revision
to PCE inflation from the BEA’s change in methodology, which
ended up being 0.3ppt in the event.
The NY Fed’s measure of consumer inflation expectations
for the year ahead meanwhile surprised to the upside, rising

from 3.58% in for to 3.90% in September, marking the highest
result so far this year. Expectations also nudged 0.1ppt higher
to 3.3% at the three-year horizon, though they were stable at
3.0% at the five-year horizon.
Local Data
The Westpac Economics New Zealand team published its
September Retail Spending Pulse , which found that retail
spending remained resilient in the face of hgher fuel prices
(see here).

日本の金融政策見通し：（井上）
米国の金融政策見通し（井上）
＜メインシナリオ（需要主導のインフレ圧力へ
シフト）：緩やかな利上げ（確率60％）＞
 FRBは、9月の見通しで、経済が力強さを増したと
評価した一方、インフレ率の2％目標への収斂に
時間を要する点を懸念
 FOMCメンバーは2026年中にも う1回の利上げを
見込む一方、2027年中は1回の利上げと現状維
持ないし利下げに2分。この間、市場は合計3回を
超える利上げを織り込み
 2026年中は、既往のエネルギー価格上昇の国内
への波及リスクに対して12月に1回の利上げを行
い、2027年には需要主導のインフレ圧力を抑制す
る目的でもう1回の利上げを行い、政策金利（FF
レートの誘導目標）は4.25～4.5％へ
 その後は、政策金利が明確に引き締め領域に入
るだけに、FRBも経済や物価の反応を見極めるた
め、政策金利を現状維持
＜代替シナリオ②（インフレ圧力が早期に沈

静化）：利上げを停止（同15％）＞
 エネルギー価格の迅速な低下によって、供給要因
による国内物価への波及が迅速に収束
 需要主導のインフレ圧力は残存するが、2026年12
月の利上げによって4％台に達する政策金利によ
り十分引き締め的と判断して現状維持に転換
 資産価格のオーバーシュートとその後の急速な調
整によって、経済の下方リスクがむしろ増大
 基調的インフレのアンカーがポイント
＜メインシナリオ（基調的インフレ率が目標に
収斂）：政策金利の速やかな中立化（確率
60％）＞
 日銀は、9月利上げの際に、経済の底堅さに自信
を示す一方、基調的インフレの上振れリスクを意識
 植田総裁は、基調的インフレを2％近傍に収斂させ
ることを主眼とする「新たな局面」に入ったとの認識
 本年12月にさらに25bpの利上げを行い、年末には
1.50％。2027年3月にも追加利上げで政策金利は
1.75％へ到達
 ただし、政策金利が明確に中立化する中で、経済
活動への負荷が徐々に明確になる可能性

 日銀によるビハインドザカーブの懸念が後退する
中で、長期金利の焦点は財政運営（消費減税の財
源等）や米国の長期金利による影響へ
＜代替シナリオ①（インフレ圧力が想定以上
に継続）：利上げを継続（同25％）＞
 景気が底堅い下で、エネルギー価格の高止まりに
よる国内物価への波及が強まりつつ継続
 インフレ期待を抑制するため、FRBは2027年中に
利上げを継続し、2027年末に5％近傍へ上昇
 経済や物価への影響だけでなく、金融環境のタイ
ト化が非直線的に出現するリスク
＜代替シナリオ②（経済の回復が想定以上に
遅延）：利上げの減速（同10％）＞
 輸出の好調が内需に波及せず、経済活動が停滞
 円安の抑制も必要であるため、日銀はより緩やかな
ペースの利上げにシフト
 日米金利差の拡大による円安圧力が輸入インフレ
→実質購買力低下→一層の内需停滞を招くリスク
＜代替シナリオ①（エネルギー価格の高騰が
想定以上に継続）：金融引き締めの領域へ（同
30％）＞
 エネルギー価格の高止まり、ないし円安の継続に

よって国内物価への波及が想定以上に長く継続
 基調的インフレの上振れを阻止するため、日銀は利
上げを継続し、ターミナルレートは2％台前半に上昇
 経済と物価のトレードオフが深刻化し、政府の経済
政策との調整が困難化

＜リスク＞
 金利急騰やAIブームの腰折れで株安と景気後退

 インフレ急進とFRBの大幅利上げで景気後退

ホームメード・インフレに発展するリスクは限定的
 労働生産性の上昇が単位労働コストを抑え 、基
調的な物価を抑制する見込み（下図）

 供給要因のインフレは27年前半に剥落へ。ただし、
堅調な米景気がディマンドプル ・イ ンフレを 招き、
27年以降も物価は2％目標に着地しない公算
＜リスク＞
 AI関連の需要増で電子部品や電力の価格を中心
にインフレ上振れ
 AIによる生産性向上のディスインフレ圧力
 イラン紛争早期終結で油価下落、インフレ下振れ
 トランプ大統領がFRBの独立性を脅かし、金融政
策の信認と物価抑制効果が低下する恐れ
 政府債務増大とドル価値の低下に伴う インフレ
＜ポイント＞
 現状の物価は政府の支援策により上値が抑えら
れている格好（上図）。同対応策の終了を想定す
れば、中東情勢悪化によるインフレ圧力により 、
コアCPIは26年度末に3％近傍に加速する公算
 今後の中東情勢の小康状態を想定し 、食料品の
消費減税による下押し効果もあり 、27年度のコア
CPIは減速に向かう と 予想。28年度以降は減税
効果剥落により 再び加速へ。減税効果を除けば 、
物価は2％目標圏内で推移すると予想

 長期インフレ予想は足下 2％程度（下図）。今後
は中東情勢悪化による影響剥落や物価目標達
成への信認から目標圏で推移と予想
＜リスク＞
 原油高や財政悪化懸念、それに伴う 円安が再度
進めば物価上振れリスク大
 食料品の消費減税に関わる政策の詳細が不透
明で、物価への影響も 不確実性が大きい
注：コアCPIは生鮮食品除くベース
インフレ率は2027年以降も2％目標に着地しない公算
出所：BEA、FRB、SFGI

（期待政策金利、％）
日本の金利見通し（宮嶋）
米国の金利見通し（渡辺）
＜ポイント＞
 日銀はインフレ上振れ防止のため利上げをペー
スアップ 。先行き は、12月・来年3月に利上げ予
想（金融政策の頁参照） 。その後の政策金利は
基調物価の2％前後の推移の下で横ばいを想定
 長期金利は 、利上げペースアップ等から 上昇に
歯止め。ただし 、米金利高や日銀国債保有減少
等から、3％近傍の高水準継続と予想（上図）
 超長期金利は世界的な財政懸念等が逆風も、発

行減額等への期待から小幅に低下へ （下図）
＜リスク＞
 想定以上のイ ンフレ加速と なれば、政策金利が
2％を超え、イールドカーブ上方シフトのリスク
 グローバルな財政懸念や高市政権の日銀利上
げ牽制姿勢が強まれば 、長・超長期ゾーンの金
利上昇が加速（スティープ化）
＜ポイント＞
 長期金利＝期待政策金利（政策金利予想）＋ター
ムプレミアム（国債保有リスク）（上図）
 タームプレミアムの上昇要因は、トランプ政策を巡
る 不確実性から、財政不安や国債の需給悪化懸
念、中間選挙後の不透明感へシフト。AI企業の大
量起債に伴う投資家資本の争奪戦も一因
 期待政策金利が上昇。イ ラ ン 紛 争 に 伴 うコ ス ト プ ッ
シュ・インフレと 堅調な経済指標がFRBを タカ 派化さ
せ、市場の利上げ期待を押し上げる（下図）
＜リスク＞
 イラン紛争早期終結で原油安、金利低下
 インフレや財政悪化、トランプ氏のFRBに対する政
治圧力等による「悪い金利上昇」
 ウォ ーシュFRB議長のフォワードガイダンス曖昧化

で、金融市場のボラティリティが上昇
 FRBの資産圧縮の加速でタームプレミアム急上昇
 金利上昇による景気後退の結果、金利下振れ
米国：長期金利＝期待政策金利＋タームプレミアム

（左軸）
ドル円の見通し（尾河）
＜ポイント＞
 前四半期から全体的にドル円の長期見通しを
小幅ながら円高方向に修正
 今年の円安トレンドのテーマであった日銀の
「利上げの遅れ（Behind the Curve）」は後退
 日米の利上げは、共にあと2回程度と 予想。日
米の金利差が著し く開く、或いは縮小はせず、

今後1 年程度は155 円を 中心とし た上下10円
程度のボックス相場が続くと 予想
 一方で、今後デジタル赤字がさらに膨らむ可
能性など 、構造的な円安要因は残るため （下
図）、その後は緩やかな円安トレンドが継続
＜リスク ＞
 日本の財政懸念が再燃することにより長期金
利の上昇を伴った円安が加速するリスク
 トランプ政策による米財政懸念により 、国債価
格、株価、ドルのトリプル安が進むリスク
 米中間選挙後も 、トランプ大統領の 「ドンロー
主義」による諸外国との摩擦が続き 、米国の
信認低下を伴うドルの大幅安が進行するなど

ユーロ円 163 179 185 191 198
＜ポイント＞
 中東情勢を受けたエネルギー価格の上昇と
物価見通し の上方修正を背景に、ECBは3年
ぶり の利上げを実施。ただユーロ圏景気は低
成長が続いており、追加利上げ余地は限定的
 ユーロドルは長期的には独米実質金利差と の
連動性を維持 。当社は26/12と 27/3の米利上
げを 予想し ており、金利差の観点から来年前
半までは上値が重い展開を予想（上図）
 その後は米利上げ局面の一巡に加え 、ドイ ツ
の国防・インフラ支出拡大 （下図）による景気
押し 上げから 金利差は縮小へ。ユーロ圏の経
常黒字も 下支えとなり、27年後半以降はユー

ロの緩やかな上昇を見込む 。独国防省予算
は27年以降大幅な増額が計画されている
＜リスク ＞
 エネルギー高の長期化やフランスの財政不安
再燃により、景気悪化や域内の信用不安が強
まれば、ユーロが大きく下振れる恐れ

連邦軍特別基金の合計。2027年は政府予算案、2028～30年は財政計画。名目GDPは2025

ソニーフィナンシャルグループ 金融市場調査部・研究員紹介
尾河 眞樹 （おがわ まき）
執行役員（金融市場調査部担当）
チーフアナリスト
ファースト ・シカゴ銀行 、JPモルガン ・
チェース銀行などの為替ディーラーを経
て、ソニー財務部にて為替リスクヘッジと

## Document 317: The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco_223e3a6a.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13025075     1

Economy 
The Institute Employment Report: September 2026 
07 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Job growth softened further in September, according to Bank of America's deposit data, with growth of 1.4% year-over-year 
(YoY), from 1.5% in August. But falling unemployment payments in September indicate a relatively healthy overall picture. 
• Lower-income households' wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap. After-tax wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY 
for lower-income households in September, while rising to 3.8% and 3.7% for middle- and higher-income households, 
respectively. 
• Lower tax withholdings and a relative strengthening in the younger/lower-income end of the labor market are driving this story. 
Additionally, average hours have risen in some lower-paid industries, which is also boosting pay packets.

Job growth slowed in September, but unemployment trends are benign  
Our estimate of job growth – which uses Bank of America deposit data to track changes in the number of customer accounts 
receiving a paycheck (see Methodology) – slowed further in September, to 1.4% year-over-year (YoY) from 1.5% YoY in August. 
The data can be noisy, partly due to seasonal variation and differences in pay-period timing, but in our view, the latest reading is 
consistent with the easing in payrolls growth seen in the September Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data (Exhibit 1). 
Still, despite slower hiring, there is little sign of a rise in joblessness in Bank of America data. Unemployment payments into 
customer accounts showed a YoY decline in September (Exhibit 2). So, overall, labor market trends appear resilient.   
Exhibit 1: September payroll growth showed further slowing in 
Bank of America deposit account data

(BLS) and Automatic Data Processing (ADP) (monthly, YoY)

After-tax wage growth cooled for lower-income, but rose for other cohorts 
Lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY in September from 4.7% YoY in August, while wage growth for middle- and 
higher-income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. Overall, these changes were modest, and lower-income 
households are continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” 
pattern we observed over much of 2025 and 2026 (Exhibit 3). 
Exhibit 3: Lower-income wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap 
After-tax wage and salary growth by household income terciles, based on Bank of America aggregated consumer deposit account data (three-month moving 
average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
In our view, the pick-up in lower- and middle-income households’ after-tax wage growth continues to reflect the impact of the 
One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBA) in reducing tax withholdings, alongside an underlying rise in job-to-job changes at the 
younger/lower-income end of the labor market (for more, read: The Institute Employment Report: August 2026). Consistent with 
that underlying pick up, BLS data shows a solid YoY rise in average hours worked in several relatively lower-paid industries, such 
as manufacturing, retail and other services. Hourly workers in these industries are likely seeing rising paychecks as a result 
(Exhibit 4).

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Estimate of payroll growth from Bank of America internal data is based on the change in customer accounts receiving a paycheck in the month. 
An adjustment is made for the difference between overall population growth and customer account growth. Occasionally historic payments can 
be reclassified, leading to revisions in the series.  
An estimate of bonus growth from Bank of America deposit data is calculated by looking at customers who have received an inbound ACH 
payroll transaction in the last two years. From this sample an estimate of bonuses is derived by looking for payroll transactions which are over 
50% higher than the median regular payroll payments received by the customer. Of these payments only those that were received around the 
same time in each of the last two years are selected.  
The job-to-job change rate (j2j rate) is defined as the proportion of customers with an identified change in their employer as a proportion of 
the total number of customers with employment income. We estimate the median pay rise associated with a j2j change using the pay in the 
first three months of the new job compared to the same three months a year ago. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

Probably 20% of the total inﬂuence. And of that, 70% reﬂects

on what the numbers say, but in the end it's a judgement call

Padhraic Garvey, CFA, Jan Frederik Slijkerman and Timothy Rahill
AI issuance pressure is having an impact on long-end rates, but so are inﬂation
and ﬁscal deﬁcits
The spending piece has a small, but not an
inconsequential impact
There are three basic AI components to account for, namely:
1. The spending piece,
2. The capital markets piece, and
3. The productivity piece.
The spending piece is relatively straightforward, as it's a straight
accounting in GDP. My colleague James Knightley has dealt with

### Page 2

this in detail here. Basically, AI accounts for a third of current
economic growth. The link here with rates is the tendency for higher
growth to be loosely associated with higher rates. However, given
that the economy outside of AI is less dynamic, the AI piece is more
of a ﬁller than anything else.
The capital markets issuance piece has a
more pursuasive impact
The capital markets piece is more complex. ING's Jan Frederik
Slijkerman did a deep dive into this space recently; it's here. For

2026, he identiﬁes TMT as a meaningful driver of additional
corporate issuance in the US, and something similar in Europe. Jan
Frederik goes on to argue that, while technology companies fund
the bulk of their investments from operating cash ﬂows, their
rapidly increasing capital spending is weighing on corporate
savings rates. As annual investment moves towards US$1 trillion,
and a chunk of this is reﬁnancing, the prognosis ahead is for
issuance volumes to remain elevated. So what you see in 2026

should at least be repeated in 2027 and beyond.
My colleagues Timothy Rahill and Marine Leleux have crunched the
latest corporate supply numbers for the US and Europe. They note
that year-to-date USD corporate issuance has hit US$878bn,
exceeding full-year corporate issuance recorded in every year from
2021 through to 2024, and running 54% above the same period
last year. The increase is driven by TMT, tripling to US$330bn. They
also note that issuers have favoured the long end of the curve. Year-

to-date issuance in maturities of 17 years and longer totals
US$235bn, making it the largest maturity bucket, ahead of 9–12
year tenors (US$219bn).
Equivalent issuance along the 10yr to 30yr part of the US Treasury
curve sums to US$340bn. That’s unchanged versus the previous
year. If we factor in buybacks, they are running at some US$20bn
above last year. So the swing factor from a US Treasuries
perspective is an eﬀective reduction in net issuance of the

aforementioned US$20bn. Contrast that with long-dated

Adding the two together, we have an overall 'delta' of some

buybacks for 2026). In other words, long-dated issuance pressure

It’s always diﬃcult to put a basis point implication of this on long
dates, but it’s tough to argue that this is not a contributing factor.
Even with less corporate issuance outside the AI space for 2026,
aggregate corporate issuance is still well up, and with a longer
duration tilt to boot. These AI-spend players have also chosen to

issue bonds in place of bank borrowing in order to get the required
size and duration. The bank avenue would have meant banks'
funding is much shorter (traditional bank duration mismatch). Bank
lending is up, but nowhere near the same scale of the increase in

bond issuance. So, either way, back ends are feeling more pressure
than they would for long-dated funding.
That all being said, the market has remained quite receptive to AI
and wider corporate issuance. New issue premiums have tended to
be higher for longer duration / AI-related issuance, but in the 10bp
area they are far from tipping the long end over the edge.
The productivity piece pushes in the same
direction, but more mythical in nature
The productivity revolution that AI promises should enhance the

productive capacity of both capital and labour. It's actually diﬃcult
to glean this from observed outcomes, but theoretically,
productivity enhancement should correlate with upward pressure
on real yields. The theoretical chain here is from higher productivity
to higher expected return on capital to higher investment spending
ﬁnanced through higher issuance, in turn commanding higher real
rates. And in terms of the productivity of labour, it's diﬃcult to

argue that productivity is not signiﬁcantly enhanced for
knowledge-based activities and for customer support, and
especially in coding generally.
The dot.com boom, from the late 1990s into 2000, has echoes in
the AI one that we see today. The 10yr real yield was at 4% back
then. And that 4% real yield coincided with a 10yr Treasury yield at
comfortably over 6%. Arguably, the productivity story today is
even more persuasive than it was back then. The current 10yr real

yield is 2.9%. And the 10yr nominal yield is at 5.2%. So we're still at
least 100bp below the levels we saw back in 2000. Whether we like
it or not, rises in real yields for productivity reasons should be
construed as higher yields for 'positive' reasons.
A suggested breakout of inﬂuences from AI
Here's a suggested breakdown of the pure AI impact.
1. About 5% from the spending piece
2. About 25% from the issuance piece

3. About 70% from the productivity piece
But we can't leave it here, as AI is not the only inﬂuencer for long-
dated rates. In fact, we'd argue that two other drivers – inﬂation
and ﬁscal deﬁcits – have been more impactful.
It's far from all about the AI impact, as
holistically we have inﬂation and ﬁscal
deﬁcit factors too
It's tough to paint a picture of long rates without
incorporating the classic inﬂuences of inﬂation and ﬁscal
deﬁcits.
On the US ﬁscal deﬁcit issue speciﬁcally, the emphasis is on

the narrative and painting a picture of debt dynamics. Back
in 2024, we did a US special on this. See it here. ING's Carsten
Brzeski, James Knightley and James Smith recently penned a
wider update here. The ﬂy in the ointment here is that US
Treasury issuance is no higher this year than last, and the
same can be said of the ﬁscal deﬁcit in cash terms. Instead,
the market is concerned with the lack of US congressional
ambition to undertake measures to improve the path for

debt dynamics. Hence, the importance of the 'narrative' does
not improve for the foreseeable future.
The inﬂation proﬁle is also key. The pandemic shocked
developed markets back into inﬂation generation capability,
and the Iran war and energy price spikes have juiced things
up again. At the same time, where we are now for the US
long yields is no more than a return to slightly above normal,
by about 50bp in the 10yr. See more here on what that is all

about. Ahead, we have inﬂation remaining sticky to the
upside for 2026, before easing lower for 2027.
What's driving higher long-end yields?

The ﬁnal breakout is as follows:
1. Inﬂation proﬁle = 50%
2. Fiscal deﬁcit narrative = 30%
3. AI piece = 20%
Contain inﬂation, and we go a long way to containing
market rates. Deﬁcits mean supply, and that's an important
ongoing inﬂuencer. And AI? Well, it's more the 'good'
(productivity) than the 'bad' (issuance) that impacts. And the

ugly? Well, that's inﬂation. Even though break-even
expectations have remained contained, printed inﬂation has
not. For the second quarter, the US GDP deﬂator (eﬀectively
inﬂation) was 4.4%, and nominal GDP rose by 6.6%. The
current 10yr yield at 5.2% is not out of kilter with that. For
more on the prognosis ahead, see here and here.

## Document 331: Top of Mind - IPO Surge_ A Red Flag for Markets_

Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc.
The US IPO market has reopened in a big way in 2026, with issuance already at a
record high. But this activity has raised two key concerns for the equity market:
whether it’s flashing a la te-cycle warning sign and whether the market can
comfortably digest so much new supply. So, how worried should investors be? The
IPO Initiative’s Jay Ritter, Acadian’s Owen Lamont, and GS’ Ben Snider agree that
we’re not in an “IPO wave” (yet) but differ on ho w much of a red flag the surge in

issuance is signaling. Snider argues that the late-cycle warning signs of past IPO
booms aren’t evident today and digestion concerns are overdone. Ritter notes that
while high new issuance volume has historically predicted lower future returns, the
signal has been weak. But Lamont is more cautious, calling an IPO wave one of the
“four horsemen” of a bubble (especially alongside a surge in debt issuance), but
stresses that IPO waves don’t necessarily signal the market top. Key to watch: firs t

day IPO returns and the all-important AI outlook.
Ben Snider, Chief US Equity Strategist, Goldman Sachs
“I’m less worried [about the surge in new issuance] than the average investor… the late-cycle warning signs that have
historically accompanied IPO booms aren’t evident today… [and] the equity supply-demand balance this year looks better
than most investors intuit.”
Jay Ritter, Director of The IPO Initiative and Professor Emeritus, The University of Florida’s Warrington College

of Business
“High new issue volume is a predictor of low future market returns. But, like most indicators, this signal works only slightly
better than chance… So, I wouldn’t view an IPO boom on its own as a sign that a market downturn is imminent.”
Owen Lamont, Senior Vice President and Portfolio Manager, Acadian Asset Management
